I know that probably isn’t the answer I’m supposed to give, but… that’s honestly what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I try to keep it sensible, or at least that’s how I tell myself it is. Most days before the early shift I’ll just have coffee, then maybe half a protein bar at lunch, and after yoga I’ll do a small bowl of vegetables. Eating more than that just makes the whole day feel… out of control. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people usually mean, like a lot of food all at once. Sometimes I feel like I’ve eaten too much even if it’s not actually much, if that makes sense. But I wouldn’t say I have episodes where I’m eating a huge amount quickly. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt really uncomfortable after eating, but I don’t make myself throw up. I know that’s probably something you need to ask, but… no, not really.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

If the scale goes up even a little, I sort of panic, like I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and end up heavy again. I know that probably sounds extreme… but that’s what happens in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently low, or do you see yourself as overweight even though others are telling you that you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I… I know other people are saying I’m underweight, and I guess I can hear that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people usually mean, like a lot of food all at once. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. If the scale goes up even a little, I get really panicky, like I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and end up heavy again. I know that probably sounds extreme… but that’s what happens in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently low, or do you see yourself as overweight even though others are telling you that you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I… I know other people are saying I’m underweight, and I guess I can hear that. But when I actually look at myself, like in the bathroom mirror or even a shop window at work, I don’t see it that way. I just get stuck on my stomach and my thighs, like they look too big, and then I feel awful about myself for the rest of the day. I know that probably isn’t the answer I’m supposed to give, but… that’s honestly what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I try to keep it sensible, or at least that’s how I tell myself it is. Most days before the early shift I’ll just have coffee, then maybe half a protein bar at lunch, and after yoga I’ll do a small bowl of vegetables. Eating more than that just makes the whole day feel… out of control. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people usually mean, like a lot of food all at once. Sometimes I feel like I’ve eaten too much even if it’s not actually much, if that makes sense. But I wouldn’t say I have episodes where I’m eating a huge amount quickly. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt really uncomfortable after eating, but I don’t make myself throw up. I know that’s probably something you need to ask, but… no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to burn off calories or prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s just to “burn off” calories exactly… I mean, exercise helps me feel like I’ve kept things in check. If the scale goes up even a little, I get really panicky, like I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and end up heavy again. So… yes, I guess I do use it to stop that from happening. Is that… does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “lost control.” I mean… I don’t really have those moments where I’m just eating and can’t stop, at least not that I can think of. Sometimes I feel upset after, like I shouldn’t have eaten something, but it’s not like I’m out of control exactly. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not what I’d consider a large amount. I mean, sometimes I worry I’ve eaten too much, but if you mean a lot of food all at once, like quickly, I don’t really have episodes like that. I hope that answers it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating much more quickly than usual during these times when you're worried about eating too much?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I haven’t really noticed eating faster than usual. If anything, I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m doing when I eat. But maybe I’m not thinking about the speed exactly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t want to say it’s the main thing, because that sounds really shallow or bad. But it does affect me a lot. Like if I catch myself in a shop window or in the bathroom mirror, I just zero in on my stomach or my thighs and think they look too big, and then it can kind of ruin how I feel about myself for the rest of the day. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you didn't think your stomach and thighs looked too big?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, I wish I could say it’s only because of how those parts look, but if the scale goes up even a little, I get really panicky. Like I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and end up heavy again. I know that maybe sounds dramatic… but that’s honestly what it feels like.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… occupational health at work asked me to come in. They’re saying my weight is dangerously low, especially with me running presses and doing fabrication all day. I guess I thought I was just getting leaner, more disciplined, you know? But they seemed pretty concerned, so… I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say I’m trying to be difficult or anything. But yes, I do get scared. If the scale goes up even a little, I sort of panic, like I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and end up heavy again. I know that probably sounds extreme… but that’s what happens in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently low, or do you see yourself as overweight even though others are telling you that you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I… I know other people are saying I’m underweight, and I guess I can hear that. But when I actually look at myself, like in the bathroom mirror or even a shop window at work, I don’t see it that way. I just get stuck on my stomach and my thighs, like they look too big, and then I feel awful about myself for the rest of the day. I know that probably isn’t the answer I’m supposed to give, but… that’s honestly what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I try to keep it sensible, or at least that’s how I tell myself it is. Most days before the early shift I’ll just have coffee, then maybe half a protein bar at lunch, and after yoga I’ll do a small bowl of vegetables. Eating more than that just makes the whole day feel… out of control. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people usually mean, like a lot of food all at once. Sometimes I feel like I’ve eaten too much even if it’s not actually much, if that makes sense. But I wouldn’t say I have episodes where I’m eating a huge amount quickly. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt really uncomfortable after eating, but I don’t make myself throw up. I know that’s probably something you need to ask, but… no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to burn off calories or prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s just to “burn off” calories exactly… I mean, exercise helps me feel like I’ve kept things in check. If the scale goes up even a little, I get really panicky, like I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and end up heavy again. So… yes, I guess I do use it to stop that from happening. Is that… does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “lost control.” I mean… I don’t really have those moments where I’m just eating and can’t stop, at least not that I can think of. Sometimes I feel upset after, like I shouldn’t have eaten something, but it’s not like I’m out of control exactly. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not what I’d consider a large amount. I mean, sometimes I worry I’ve eaten too much, but if you mean a lot of food all at once, like quickly, I don’t really have episodes like that. I hope that answers it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating much more quickly than usual during these times when you're worried about eating too much?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I haven’t really noticed eating faster than usual. If anything, I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m doing when I eat. But maybe I’m not thinking about the speed exactly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t want to say it’s the main thing, because that sounds really shallow or bad. But it does affect me a lot. Like if I catch myself in a shop window or in the bathroom mirror, I just zero in on my stomach or my thighs and think they look too big, and then it can kind of ruin how I feel about myself for the rest of the day. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you didn't think your stomach and thighs looked too big?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, I wish I could say it’s only because of how those parts look, but if the scale goes up even a little, I get really panicky. Like I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and end up heavy again. I know that maybe sounds dramatic… but that’s honestly what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive in a short period of time, even if it was just once?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so. Not in the way that sounds, anyway. I mean, sometimes I might feel like I ate “too much,” but that could be something other people would probably think is normal, so I’m not sure it counts. I wouldn’t want to exaggerate it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you ate a normal or small amount of food, but still felt uncomfortably full afterwards?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I can feel full sometimes after eating, but I don’t know if it’s “uncomfortably” full, or if I’m just paying too much attention to it. I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. What would count as that?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I wouldn’t want to exaggerate it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you ate a normal or small amount of food, but still felt uncomfortably full afterwards?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I can feel full sometimes after eating, but I don’t know if it’s “uncomfortably” full, or if I’m just paying too much attention to it. I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. What would count as that?\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever felt like you couldn't stop eating once you started?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 45 / metal_worker_or_plastic_worker / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… occupational health at work asked me to come in. They’re saying my weight is dangerously low, especially with me running presses and doing fabrication all day. I guess I thought I was just getting leaner, more disciplined, you know? But they seemed pretty concerned, so… I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say I’m trying to be difficult or anything. But yes, I do get scared. If the scale goes up even a little, I sort of panic, like I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and end up heavy again. I know that probably sounds extreme… but that’s what happens in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently low, or do you see yourself as overweight even though others are telling you that you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I… I know other people are saying I’m underweight, and I guess I can hear that. But when I actually look at myself, like in the bathroom mirror or even a shop window at work, I don’t see it that way. I just get stuck on my stomach and my thighs, like they look too big, and then I feel awful about myself for the rest of the day. I know that probably isn’t the answer I’m supposed to give, but… that’s honestly what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I try to keep it sensible, or at least that’s how I tell myself it is. Most days before the early shift I’ll just have coffee, then maybe half a protein bar at lunch, and after yoga I’ll do a small bowl of vegetables. Eating more than that just makes the whole day feel… out of control. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people usually mean, like a lot of food all at once. Sometimes I feel like I’ve eaten too much even if it’s not actually much, if that makes sense. But I wouldn’t say I have episodes where I’m eating a huge amount quickly. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt really uncomfortable after eating, but I don’t make myself throw up. I know that’s probably something you need to ask, but… no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to burn off calories or prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s just to “burn off” calories exactly… I mean, exercise helps me feel like I’ve kept things in check. If the scale goes up even a little, I get really panicky, like I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and end up heavy again. So… yes, I guess I do use it to stop that from happening. Is that… does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “lost control.” I mean… I don’t really have those moments where I’m just eating and can’t stop, at least not that I can think of. Sometimes I feel upset after, like I shouldn’t have eaten something, but it’s not like I’m out of control exactly. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not what I’d consider a large amount. I mean, sometimes I worry I’ve eaten too much, but if you mean a lot of food all at once, like quickly, I don’t really have episodes like that. I hope that answers it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating much more quickly than usual during these times when you're worried about eating too much?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I haven’t really noticed eating faster than usual. If anything, I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m doing when I eat. But maybe I’m not thinking about the speed exactly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t want to say it’s the main thing, because that sounds really shallow or bad. But it does affect me a lot. Like if I catch myself in a shop window or in the bathroom mirror, I just zero in on my stomach or my thighs and think they look too big, and then it can kind of ruin how I feel about myself for the rest of the day. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you didn't think your stomach and thighs looked too big?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, I wish I could say it’s only because of how those parts look, but if the scale goes up even a little, I get really panicky. Like I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and end up heavy again. I know that maybe sounds dramatic… but that’s honestly what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive in a short period of time, even if it was just once?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so. Not in the way that sounds, anyway. I mean, sometimes I might feel like I ate “too much,” but that could be something other people would probably think is normal, so I’m not sure it counts. I wouldn’t want to exaggerate it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you ate a normal or small amount of food, but still felt uncomfortably full afterwards?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I can feel full sometimes after eating, but I don’t know if it’s “uncomfortably” full, or if I’m just paying too much attention to it. I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. What would count as that?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn't stop eating once you started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, once I decide I’m done, I can usually stop. Sometimes I worry that I might not, if that makes sense, but I can’t think of a time where I truly couldn’t stop eating once I started. I know that probably isn’t the answer I’m supposed to give, but… that’s honestly what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I try to keep it sensible, or at least that’s how I tell myself it is. Most days before the early shift I’ll just have coffee, then maybe half a protein bar at lunch, and after yoga I’ll do a small bowl of vegetables. Eating more than that just makes the whole day feel… out of control. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people usually mean, like a lot of food all at once. Sometimes I feel like I’ve eaten too much even if it’s not actually much, if that makes sense. But I wouldn’t say I have episodes where I’m eating a huge amount quickly. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt really uncomfortable after eating, but I don’t make myself throw up. I know that’s probably something you need to ask, but… no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to burn off calories or prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s just to “burn off” calories exactly… I mean, exercise helps me feel like I’ve kept things in check. If the scale goes up even a little, I get really panicky, like I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and end up heavy again. So… yes, I guess I do use it to stop that from happening. Is that… does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “lost control.” I mean… I don’t really have those moments where I’m just eating and can’t stop, at least not that I can think of. Sometimes I feel upset after, like I shouldn’t have eaten something, but it’s not like I’m out of control exactly. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not what I’d consider a large amount. I mean, sometimes I worry I’ve eaten too much, but if you mean a lot of food all at once, like quickly, I don’t really have episodes like that. I hope that answers it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating much more quickly than usual during these times when you're worried about eating too much?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I haven’t really noticed eating faster than usual. If anything, I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m doing when I eat. But maybe I’m not thinking about the speed exactly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t want to say it’s the main thing, because that sounds really shallow or bad. But it does affect me a lot. Like if I catch myself in a shop window or in the bathroom mirror, I just zero in on my stomach or my thighs and think they look too big, and then it can kind of ruin how I feel about myself for the rest of the day. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you didn't think your stomach and thighs looked too big?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, I wish I could say it’s only because of how those parts look, but if the scale goes up even a little, I get really panicky. Like I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and end up heavy again. I know that maybe sounds dramatic… but that’s honestly what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive in a short period of time, even if it was just once?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so. Not in the way that sounds, anyway. I mean, sometimes I might feel like I ate “too much,” but that could be something other people would probably think is normal, so I’m not sure it counts. I wouldn’t want to exaggerate it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you ate a normal or small amount of food, but still felt uncomfortably full afterwards?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I can feel full sometimes after eating, but I don’t know if it’s “uncomfortably” full, or if I’m just paying too much attention to it. I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. What would count as that?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn't stop eating once you started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, once I decide I’m done, I can usually stop. Sometimes I worry that I might not, if that makes sense, but I can’t think of a time where I truly couldn’t stop eating once I started. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating without being able to stop once you've started, even if it's not a lot of food?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in a “I can’t stop” way. Sometimes I might feel a little nervous once I’ve started eating, like I’m watching myself too closely, but I can still stop. I’m sorry if that’s not a very clear answer.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

They’re saying my weight is dangerously low, especially with me running presses and doing fabrication all day. I guess I thought I was just getting leaner, more disciplined, you know? But they seemed pretty concerned, so… I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say I’m trying to be difficult or anything. But yes, I do get scared. If the scale goes up even a little, I sort of panic, like I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and end up heavy again. I know that probably sounds extreme… but that’s what happens in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently low, or do you see yourself as overweight even though others are telling you that you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I… I know other people are saying I’m underweight, and I guess I can hear that. But when I actually look at myself, like in the bathroom mirror or even a shop window at work, I don’t see it that way. I just get stuck on my stomach and my thighs, like they look too big, and then I feel awful about myself for the rest of the day. I know that probably isn’t the answer I’m supposed to give, but… that’s honestly what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I try to keep it sensible, or at least that’s how I tell myself it is. Most days before the early shift I’ll just have coffee, then maybe half a protein bar at lunch, and after yoga I’ll do a small bowl of vegetables. Eating more than that just makes the whole day feel… out of control. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people usually mean, like a lot of food all at once. Sometimes I feel like I’ve eaten too much even if it’s not actually much, if that makes sense. But I wouldn’t say I have episodes where I’m eating a huge amount quickly. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt really uncomfortable after eating, but I don’t make myself throw up. I know that’s probably something you need to ask, but… no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to burn off calories or prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s just to “burn off” calories exactly… I mean, exercise helps me feel like I’ve kept things in check. If the scale goes up even a little, I get really panicky, like I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and end up heavy again. So… yes, I guess I do use it to stop that from happening. Is that… does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “lost control.” I mean… I don’t really have those moments where I’m just eating and can’t stop, at least not that I can think of. Sometimes I feel upset after, like I shouldn’t have eaten something, but it’s not like I’m out of control exactly. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not what I’d consider a large amount. I mean, sometimes I worry I’ve eaten too much, but if you mean a lot of food all at once, like quickly, I don’t really have episodes like that. I hope that answers it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating much more quickly than usual during these times when you're worried about eating too much?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I haven’t really noticed eating faster than usual. If anything, I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m doing when I eat. But maybe I’m not thinking about the speed exactly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t want to say it’s the main thing, because that sounds really shallow or bad. But it does affect me a lot. Like if I catch myself in a shop window or in the bathroom mirror, I just zero in on my stomach or my thighs and think they look too big, and then it can kind of ruin how I feel about myself for the rest of the day. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you didn't think your stomach and thighs looked too big?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, I wish I could say it’s only because of how those parts look, but if the scale goes up even a little, I get really panicky. Like I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and end up heavy again. I know that maybe sounds dramatic… but that’s honestly what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive in a short period of time, even if it was just once?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so. Not in the way that sounds, anyway. I mean, sometimes I might feel like I ate “too much,” but that could be something other people would probably think is normal, so I’m not sure it counts. I wouldn’t want to exaggerate it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you ate a normal or small amount of food, but still felt uncomfortably full afterwards?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I can feel full sometimes after eating, but I don’t know if it’s “uncomfortably” full, or if I’m just paying too much attention to it. I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. What would count as that?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn't stop eating once you started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, once I decide I’m done, I can usually stop. Sometimes I worry that I might not, if that makes sense, but I can’t think of a time where I truly couldn’t stop eating once I started. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating without being able to stop once you've started, even if it's not a lot of food?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in a “I can’t stop” way. Sometimes I might feel a little nervous once I’ve started eating, like I’m watching myself too closely, but I can still stop. I’m sorry if that’s not a very clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive, even if it was just one time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not what I would call excessive. I mean, I might feel like I ate too much sometimes, but when I think about the actual amount, it probably wasn’t a lot. I’m sorry, I don’t know if that answers it the way you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you are eating an amount of food that is much larger than what most people would eat in a similar time period?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, sometimes it feels like a lot to me, but if I picture what other people would eat in the same amount of time, I don’t think it would be more than that. Maybe less, honestly. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I know that’s probably something you need to ask, but… no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to burn off calories or prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s just to “burn off” calories exactly… I mean, exercise helps me feel like I’ve kept things in check. If the scale goes up even a little, I get really panicky, like I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and end up heavy again. So… yes, I guess I do use it to stop that from happening. Is that… does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “lost control.” I mean… I don’t really have those moments where I’m just eating and can’t stop, at least not that I can think of. Sometimes I feel upset after, like I shouldn’t have eaten something, but it’s not like I’m out of control exactly. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not what I’d consider a large amount. I mean, sometimes I worry I’ve eaten too much, but if you mean a lot of food all at once, like quickly, I don’t really have episodes like that. I hope that answers it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating much more quickly than usual during these times when you're worried about eating too much?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I haven’t really noticed eating faster than usual. If anything, I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m doing when I eat. But maybe I’m not thinking about the speed exactly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t want to say it’s the main thing, because that sounds really shallow or bad. But it does affect me a lot. Like if I catch myself in a shop window or in the bathroom mirror, I just zero in on my stomach or my thighs and think they look too big, and then it can kind of ruin how I feel about myself for the rest of the day. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you didn't think your stomach and thighs looked too big?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, I wish I could say it’s only because of how those parts look, but if the scale goes up even a little, I get really panicky. Like I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and end up heavy again. I know that maybe sounds dramatic… but that’s honestly what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive in a short period of time, even if it was just once?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so. Not in the way that sounds, anyway. I mean, sometimes I might feel like I ate “too much,” but that could be something other people would probably think is normal, so I’m not sure it counts. I wouldn’t want to exaggerate it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you ate a normal or small amount of food, but still felt uncomfortably full afterwards?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I can feel full sometimes after eating, but I don’t know if it’s “uncomfortably” full, or if I’m just paying too much attention to it. I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. What would count as that?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn't stop eating once you started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, once I decide I’m done, I can usually stop. Sometimes I worry that I might not, if that makes sense, but I can’t think of a time where I truly couldn’t stop eating once I started. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating without being able to stop once you've started, even if it's not a lot of food?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in a “I can’t stop” way. Sometimes I might feel a little nervous once I’ve started eating, like I’m watching myself too closely, but I can still stop. I’m sorry if that’s not a very clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive, even if it was just one time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not what I would call excessive. I mean, I might feel like I ate too much sometimes, but when I think about the actual amount, it probably wasn’t a lot. I’m sorry, I don’t know if that answers it the way you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you are eating an amount of food that is much larger than what most people would eat in a similar time period?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, sometimes it feels like a lot to me, but if I picture what other people would eat in the same amount of time, I don’t think it would be more than that. Maybe less, honestly. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you felt like you were eating an unusually large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had an episode like that, no. I mean… I can feel like it was too much because I get anxious about it, but I don’t think it was an unusually large amount in a short time. At least not compared to what most people would mean by that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I know that’s probably something you need to ask, but… no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to burn off calories or prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s just to “burn off” calories exactly… I mean, exercise helps me feel like I’ve kept things in check. If the scale goes up even a little, I get really panicky, like I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and end up heavy again. So… yes, I guess I do use it to stop that from happening. Is that… does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “lost control.” I mean… I don’t really have those moments where I’m just eating and can’t stop, at least not that I can think of. Sometimes I feel upset after, like I shouldn’t have eaten something, but it’s not like I’m out of control exactly. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not what I’d consider a large amount. I mean, sometimes I worry I’ve eaten too much, but if you mean a lot of food all at once, like quickly, I don’t really have episodes like that. I hope that answers it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating much more quickly than usual during these times when you're worried about eating too much?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I haven’t really noticed eating faster than usual. If anything, I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m doing when I eat. But maybe I’m not thinking about the speed exactly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t want to say it’s the main thing, because that sounds really shallow or bad. But it does affect me a lot. Like if I catch myself in a shop window or in the bathroom mirror, I just zero in on my stomach or my thighs and think they look too big, and then it can kind of ruin how I feel about myself for the rest of the day. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you didn't think your stomach and thighs looked too big?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, I wish I could say it’s only because of how those parts look, but if the scale goes up even a little, I get really panicky. Like I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and end up heavy again. I know that maybe sounds dramatic… but that’s honestly what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive in a short period of time, even if it was just once?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so. Not in the way that sounds, anyway. I mean, sometimes I might feel like I ate “too much,” but that could be something other people would probably think is normal, so I’m not sure it counts. I wouldn’t want to exaggerate it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you ate a normal or small amount of food, but still felt uncomfortably full afterwards?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I can feel full sometimes after eating, but I don’t know if it’s “uncomfortably” full, or if I’m just paying too much attention to it. I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. What would count as that?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn't stop eating once you started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, once I decide I’m done, I can usually stop. Sometimes I worry that I might not, if that makes sense, but I can’t think of a time where I truly couldn’t stop eating once I started. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating without being able to stop once you've started, even if it's not a lot of food?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in a “I can’t stop” way. Sometimes I might feel a little nervous once I’ve started eating, like I’m watching myself too closely, but I can still stop. I’m sorry if that’s not a very clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you considered excessive, even if it was just one time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not what I would call excessive. I mean, I might feel like I ate too much sometimes, but when I think about the actual amount, it probably wasn’t a lot. I’m sorry, I don’t know if that answers it the way you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you are eating an amount of food that is much larger than what most people would eat in a similar time period?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, sometimes it feels like a lot to me, but if I picture what other people would eat in the same amount of time, I don’t think it would be more than that. Maybe less, honestly. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you felt like you were eating an unusually large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had an episode like that, no. I mean… I can feel like it was too much because I get anxious about it, but I don’t think it was an unusually large amount in a short time. At least not compared to what most people would mean by that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you felt like you lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it losing control… I mean, I get upset with myself sometimes, or anxious while I’m eating, but I don’t think it’s like I can’t stop or I’m out of control. At least, not in the way I think you mean. Is that okay to say?"
